The added responsibility initially seemed to energize her; she reorganized her sister’s home, took on extra consulting work, and launched a small online shop for her pottery late at night, telling friends she finally felt \"in her flow.\" Over time, the travel, disrupted sleep, and constant juggling between caregiving and work led to a marked slump—she started missing bill payments more often, canceling art fairs, and feeling paralyzed when trying to plan even simple store-layout projects. After about two months of this decline, a longtime client gently pointed out that her work had become uncharacteristically scattered, and combined with her own worry about her fading mental sharpness, she agreed to be evaluated by a psychiatrist referred through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind feels foggy and I can’t seem to focus or make decisions the way I used to.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Most days lately I wake up with this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest, like someone flipped a switch and drained all the color out to bed or the TV. \n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My husband keeps asking if I’m okay because even walking from the car to my sister’s front door feels like I’m moving through molasses, and I sit at \n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Lately my brain feels like it’s full of fog, so that even choosing which display to put near the entrance of a store or deciding what route to take to my sister’s easier for everyone if I just didn’t wake up one morning, and while I haven’t made any plans to hurt myself, those kinds of thoughts have been popping up more often and lingering. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Back when my sister first got sick and I threw myself into rearranging her house and taking on extra clients, I felt unusually upbeat and wired, like I was on a roll and nothing could faze me, but I’d also snap at people over the smallest things if they slowed me down. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that busy stretch I was staying up half the night reorganizing her closets, drafting new proposals for clients, and tinkering with my pottery website because I felt like I had endless energy and couldn’t bear the idea of just sitting still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): I remember telling my friends, only half joking, that I was the only one who could really get my sister’s life and my business in order because I was so efficient and creative, like I had some special knack other people just didn’t have. \n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I was in that more revved‑up phase, I couldn’t stay focused on one thing—I'd be working on a merchandising plan and then a text ping, a bird outside the window, or a new idea about reorganizing my sister’s pantry would pull me off track, and I’d jump from one task to another without finishing. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I packed my days with back‑to‑back client meetings, constant phone calls checking on my sister, and late‑night pottery and website updates, piling on more projects than I could reasonably handle because it felt like I had to keep doing and fixing everything at once.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last couple of months I’ve had to turn down new consulting projects, postpone work for longtime clients, and lean on my husband to handle things like paying bills and keeping the house in order because I just can’t keep up the way I used to.\n- duration: Looking back, these big swings from feeling overly revved up and driven to months of feeling foggy and drained have been coming and going for several The added responsibility initially seemed to energize her; she reorganized her sister’s home, took on extra consulting work, and launched a small online shop for her pottery late at night, telling friends she finally felt \"in her flow.\" Over time, the travel, disrupted sleep, and constant juggling between caregiving and work led to a marked slump—she started missing bill payments more often, canceling art fairs, and feeling paralyzed when trying to plan even simple store-layout projects. After about two months of this decline, a longtime client gently pointed out that her work had become uncharacteristically scattered, and combined with her own worry about her fading mental sharpness, she agreed to be evaluated by a psychiatrist referred through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind feels foggy and I can’t seem to focus or make decisions the way I used to.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Most days lately I wake up with this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest, like someone flipped a switch and drained all the color out of my life, and it just hangs over me no matter whether I'm at my sister’s house or sitting at my own kitchen table. \n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing a new batch of mugs for the online shop, but for the last couple of months I just stare at the plans or the clay and feel nothing, so I end up turning off the light and going back to bed or the TV. \n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My husband keeps asking if I’m okay because even walking from the car to my sister’s front door feels like I’m moving through molasses, and I sit at the computer forever just staring before I can make my fingers type an email. \n- S029 (Fatigue): No matter how much I sleep—even on the days I don’t drive to West Texas—I feel wrung out, like I’ve already worked a full shift before I’ve even had my coffee, and simple things like folding laundry wear me out. \n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep beating myself up, thinking I’m a terrible sister and consultant because I can’t keep up with her care or my clients the way I promised, and I replay every little mistake—like a missed bill or a late report—as proof that I’m letting everyone down. \n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Lately my brain feels like it’s full of fog, so that even choosing which display to put near the entrance of a store or deciding what route to take to my sister’s cardiology appointment can leave me stuck and second-guessing myself for far longer than it should. \n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On the drives back from my sister’s, I catch myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn’t wake up one morning, and while I haven’t made any plans to hurt myself, those kinds of thoughts have been popping up more often and lingering. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Back when my sister first got sick and I threw myself into rearranging her house and taking on extra clients, I felt unusually upbeat and wired, like I was on a roll and nothing could faze me, but I’d also snap at people over the smallest things if they slowed me down. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that busy stretch I was staying up half the night reorganizing her closets, drafting new proposals for clients, and tinkering with my pottery website because I felt like I had endless energy and couldn’t bear the idea of just sitting still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): I remember telling my friends, only half joking, that I was the only one who could really get my sister’s life and my business in order because I was so efficient and creative, like I had some special knack other people just didn’t have. \n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I was in that more revved‑up phase, I couldn’t stay focused on one thing—I'd be working on a merchandising plan and then a text ping, a bird outside the window, or a new idea about reorganizing my sister’s pantry would pull me off track, and I’d jump from one task to another without finishing. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I packed my days with back‑to‑back client meetings, constant phone calls checking on my sister, and late‑night pottery and website updates, piling on more projects than I could reasonably handle because it felt like I had to keep doing and fixing everything at once.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last couple of months I’ve had to turn down new consulting projects, postpone work for longtime clients, and lean on my husband to handle things like paying bills and keeping the house in order because I just can’t keep up the way I used to.\n- duration: Looking back, these big swings from feeling overly revved up and driven to months of feeling foggy and drained have been coming and going for several Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Most days lately I wake up with this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest, like someone flipped a switch and drained all the color out of my life, and it just hangs over me no matter whether I'm at my sister’s house or sitting at my own kitchen table. \n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing a new batch of mugs for the online shop, but for the last couple of months I just stare at the plans or the clay and feel nothing, so I end up turning off the light and going back to bed or the TV. \n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My husband keeps asking if I’m okay because even walking from the car to my sister’s front door feels like I’m moving through molasses, and I sit at the computer forever just staring before I can make my fingers type an email. \n- S029 (Fatigue): No matter how much I sleep—even on the days I don’t drive to West Texas—I feel wrung out, like I’ve already worked a full shift before I’ve even had my coffee, and simple things like folding laundry wear me out. \n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep beating myself up, thinking I’m a terrible sister and consultant because I can’t keep up with her care or my clients the way I promised, and I replay every little mistake—like a missed bill or a late report—as proof that I’m letting everyone down. \n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Lately my brain feels like it’s full of fog, so that even choosing which display to put near the entrance of a store or deciding what route to take to my sister’s cardiology appointment can leave me stuck and second-guessing myself for far longer than it should. \n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On the drives back from my sister’s, I catch myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn’t wake up one morning, and while I haven’t made any plans to hurt myself, those kinds of thoughts have been popping up more often and lingering. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Back when my sister first got sick and I threw myself into rearranging her house and taking on extra clients, I felt unusually upbeat and wired, like I was on a roll and nothing could faze me, but I’d also snap at people over the smallest things if they slowed me down. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that busy stretch I was staying up half the night reorganizing her closets, drafting new proposals for clients, and tinkering with my pottery website because I felt like I had endless energy and couldn’t bear the idea of just sitting still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): I remember telling my friends, only half joking, that I was the only one who could really get my sister’s life and my business in order because I was so efficient and creative, like I had some special knack other people just didn’t have. \n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I was in that more revved‑up phase, I couldn’t stay focused on one thing—I'd be working on a merchandising plan and then a text ping, a bird outside the window, or a new idea about reorganizing my sister’s pantry would pull me off track, and I’d jump from one task to another without finishing. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I packed my days with back‑to‑back client meetings, constant phone calls checking on my sister, and late‑night pottery and website updates, piling on more projects than I could reasonably handle because it felt like I had to keep doing and fixing everything at once.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last couple can’t keep up the way I used to.\n- duration: Looking back, these big swings from feeling overly revved up and driven to months of feeling foggy and drained have been coming and going for several years, but this current stretch started after my sister’s heart problems worsened.\n- depressive_episode_duration: This most recent low period has been dragging on for about six months now, ever since After about two months of this decline, a longtime client gently pointed out that her work had become uncharacteristically scattered, and combined with her own worry about her fading mental sharpness, The added responsibility initially seemed to energize her; she reorganized her sister’s home, took on extra consulting work, and launched a small online shop for her pottery late at night, telling friends she finally felt \"in her flow.\" Over time, the travel, disrupted sleep, and constant juggling between caregiving and work led to a marked slump—she started missing bill payments more often, canceling art fairs, and feeling paralyzed when trying to plan even simple store-layout projects. After about two months of this decline, a longtime client gently pointed out that her work had become uncharacteristically scattered, and combined with her own worry about her fading mental sharpness, she agreed to be evaluated by a psychiatrist referred through her primary care clinic.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind feels foggy and I can’t seem to focus or make decisions the way I used to.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Most days lately I wake up with this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest, like someone flipped a switch and drained all the color out of my life, and it just hangs over me no matter whether I'm at my sister’s house or sitting at my own kitchen table. \n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing a new batch of mugs for the online shop, but for the last couple of months I just stare at the plans or the clay and feel nothing, so I end up turning off the light and going back to bed or the TV. \n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): My husband keeps asking if I’m okay because even walking from the car to my sister’s front door feels like I’m moving through molasses, and I sit at the computer forever just staring before I can make my fingers type an email. \n- S029 (Fatigue): No matter how much I sleep—even on the days I don’t drive to West Texas—I feel wrung out, like I’ve already worked a full shift before I’ve even had my coffee, and simple things like folding laundry wear me out. \n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep beating myself up, thinking I’m a terrible sister and consultant because I can’t keep up with her care or my clients the way I promised, and I replay every little mistake—like a missed bill or a late report—as proof that I’m letting everyone down. \n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Lately my brain feels like it’s full of fog, so that even choosing which display to put near the entrance of a store or deciding what route to take to my sister’s cardiology appointment can leave me stuck and second-guessing myself for far longer than it should. \n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On the drives back from my sister’s, I catch myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn’t wake up one morning, and while I haven’t made any plans to hurt myself, those kinds of thoughts have been popping up more often and lingering. \n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): Back when my sister first got sick and I threw myself into rearranging her house and taking on extra clients, I felt unusually upbeat and wired, like I was on a roll and nothing could faze me, but I’d also snap at people over the smallest things if they slowed me down. \n- S034 (Increased_Activity): During that busy stretch I was staying up half the night reorganizing her closets, drafting new proposals for clients, and tinkering with my pottery website because I felt like I had endless energy and couldn’t bear the idea of just sitting still. \n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): I remember telling my friends, only half joking, that I was the only one who could really get my sister’s life and my business in order because I was so efficient and creative, like I had some special knack other people just didn’t have. \n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I was in that more revved‑up phase, I couldn’t stay focused on one thing—I'd be working on a merchandising plan and then a text ping, a bird outside the window, or a new idea about reorganizing my sister’s pantry would pull me off track, and I’d jump from one task to another without finishing. \n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): I packed my days with back‑to‑back client meetings, constant phone calls checking on my sister, and late‑night pottery and website updates, piling on more projects than I could reasonably handle because it felt like I had to keep doing and fixing everything at once.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last couple of months I’ve had to turn down new consulting projects, postpone work for longtime clients, and lean on my husband to handle things like paying bills and keeping the house in order because I just can’t keep up the way I used to.\n- duration: Looking back, these big swings from feeling overly revved up and driven to months of feeling foggy and drained have been coming and going for several years, but this current stretch started after my sister’s heart problems worsened.\n- depressive_episode_duration: This most recent low period has been dragging on for about six months now, ever since Most days I wake up with this heavy, sinking feeling in my chest, like someone flipped a switch and drained all the color out of my life. It just hangs over me whether I'm at my sister’s house or sitting at my own kitchen table.\n\nDoctor: Did the fogginess and trouble concentrating start before you became depressed, or did they begin after the low mood started?\n\nPatient: They actually seemed to start at the exact same time. About six months ago, when my initial high energy wore off and I hit this heavy slump, the low mood and the brain fog rolled in together. It felt like they were both part of the same downward slide.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've completely lost interest in the things I used to love. I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing a new batch of mugs for my online shop, but lately, I just stare at the plans or the clay, feel completely empty, and end up turning off the light to go back to bed or the TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had changes in sleep or appetite, felt slowed down or agitated, or had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I do feel incredibly slowed down, like I'm constantly moving through molasses, and I'm just exhausted no matter how much I sleep. And... on the drives back from my sister's, I've caught myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn't wake up, though I haven't made any plans to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or made any plans or preparations to end your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't made any plans or preparations to hurt myself. I felt like I had a special knack and couldn't sit still, so I took on extra clients, though I also got easily distracted and snapped at anyone who slowed me down.\n\nDoctor: During your depressed periods, have you ever heard, seen, or strongly believed things that other people said were not real, such as voices, messages, or feeling certain that you were being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like that. I just feel so overwhelmed and unable to manage my own life right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least several days when you felt unusually energized or overly confident, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or did things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes, when my sister first got sick, I went through a phase where I felt incredibly upbeat, wired, and had endless energy, staying up half the night to reorganize her house and launch an online pottery shop. I felt like I had a special knack and couldn't sit still, so I took on extra clients, though I also got easily distracted and snapped at anyone who slowed me down.\n\nDoctor: During your depressed periods, have you ever heard, seen, or strongly believed things that other people said were not real, such as voices, messages, or feeling certain that you were being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like that. I have never heard or seen things that weren't really there, nor have I believed things that others told me weren't real.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did it ever become so severe that you needed to be hospitalized, or did you have hallucinations or fixed false beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, it never got quite that extreme. I was never hospitalized, and I didn't experience any hallucinations or false beliefs during that high-energy period. I was just very revved up and busy, but I was still grounded in reality."

About six months ago, when my initial high energy wore off and I hit this heavy slump, the low mood and the brain fog rolled in together. It felt like they were both part of the same downward slide.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies, socializing, or work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've completely lost interest in the things I used to love. I used to get so much joy out of sketching store layouts or throwing a new batch of mugs for my online shop, but lately, I just stare at the plans or the clay, feel completely empty, and end up turning off the light to go back to bed or the TV.\n\nDoctor: Have you also had changes in sleep or appetite, felt slowed down or agitated, or had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I do feel incredibly slowed down, like I'm constantly moving through molasses, and I'm just exhausted no matter how much I sleep. And... on the drives back from my sister's, I've caught myself thinking it might be easier for everyone if I just didn't wake up, though I haven't made any plans to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself, or made any plans or preparations to end your life?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't made any plans or preparations to hurt myself. It’s just that those thoughts of it being easier if I didn't wake up have been popping up and lingering more often lately, especially on the long drives back from my sister’s. But I don't have any intention of actually doing anything.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had any thoughts that you might act on those thoughts, or any plan or intent to hurt yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had any thoughts of acting on it, and I definitely don't have any plan or intent to hurt myself. It's really just those passive thoughts about how it would be easier if I didn't wake up in the morning. I would never do anything to harm myself.\n\nDoctor: How much have these symptoms affected your ability to work, take care of yourself, or do daily activities? Have you been able to function at all, or has it become very difficult to do even basic tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s been incredibly hard to keep up, and I've had to turn down new consulting projects and postpone work for my longtime clients. I’ve also had to rely heavily on my husband to handle basic things like paying the bills and keeping the house in order. I just feel so terrible because I simply can’t manage my own life right now.\n\nDoctor: How much have these symptoms affected your ability to work, take care of yourself, or do daily activities? Have you been able to function at all, or has it become very difficult to do even basic tasks?\n\nPatient: It really has become incredibly difficult to keep up with anything. I've had to turn down new consulting projects, put my longtime clients on hold, and rely entirely on my husband to handle daily tasks like paying bills and keeping the house in order. I just feel so overwhelmed and unable to manage my own life right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least several days when you felt unusually energized or overly confident, needed much less sleep, talked more than usual, or did things that were out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes, when my sister first got sick, I went through a phase where I felt incredibly upbeat, wired, and had endless energy, staying up half the night to reorganize her house and launch an online pottery shop. I felt like I had a special knack and couldn't sit still, so I took on extra clients, though I also got easily distracted and snapped at anyone who slowed me down.\n\nDoctor: During your depressed periods, have you ever heard, seen, or strongly believed things that other people said were not real, such as voices, messages, or feeling certain that you were being punished or watched?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like that. I have never heard or seen things that weren't really there, nor have I believed things that others told me weren't real.\n\nDoctor: During that high-energy period, did it ever become so severe that you needed to be hospitalized, or did you have hallucinations or fixed false beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, it never got quite that extreme. I was never hospitalized, and I didn't experience any hallucinations or false beliefs during that high-energy period. I was just very revved up and busy, but I was still grounded in reality.\n\nDoctor: During this depressed period, have your symptoms been so severe that you can still do some of your usual activities, or have they made it nearly impossible to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: It has indeed made functioning nearly impossible for me. I've had to turn down new consulting projects, postpone work for my longtime clients, and rely heavily on my husband to handle household tasks and pay the bills. I simply cannot keep up with my normal life anymore."

The added responsibility initially seemed to energize her; she reorganized her sister’s home, took on extra consulting work, and launched a small online shop for her pottery late at night, telling friends she finally felt \"in her flow.\" Over time, the travel, disrupted sleep, and constant juggling between caregiving and work led to a marked slump—she started missing bill payments more often, canceling art fairs, and feeling paralyzed when trying to plan even simple store-layout projects. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
